Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:3:ecdc41bd9058fb89',
      text='## 2. Preliminaries\n'
           '\n'
           'Agent Definition We consider a general interactive setting where an agent solves tasks by acting in an '
           'environment. An\n'
           'environment is defined as $\\mathcal { E } = ( \\mathcal { S } , \\mathcal { A } , \\mathcal { P } )$ , '
           'where A is the set\n'
           'of executable actions, S is the set of observable states, and $\\mathcal { P } ( s ^ { \\prime } \\mid s , '
           'a )$ is the\n'
           'transition dynamics. At time step t, the agent receives an observation $o _ { t } \\in \\mathcal { O }$ '
           'and produces an\n'
           'action $a _ { t } \\in \\mathcal A$ . Following the ReAct style formulation, the agent therefore selects '
           'an\n'
           'action $\\hat { a } _ { t } \\in \\hat { \\mathcal { A } }$ conditioned on its\n'
           'context $c _ { t } = \\left( o _ { 1 } , \\hat { a } _ { 1 } , \\dots , o _ { t - 1 } , \\hat { a } _ { t '
           '- 1 } , o _ { t } \\right)$\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\hat {a} _ {t} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot \\mid c _ {t}), \\qquad \\hat {a} _ {t} \\in \\hat {\\mathcal '
           '{A}}.\\tag{1}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'Executing $\\hat { a } _ { t } \\in \\mathcal A$ yields a new observation via the environment. The final '
           'trajectory\n'
           'is $\\tau = ( o _ { 1 } , \\hat { a } _ { 1 } , \\dots , o _ { T } , \\hat { a } _ { T } )$\n'
           '\n'
           'LLM Agent and Skill-Conditioned Execution. Let Q be the tasks set. We write $q \\in \\mathcal { Q }$ for '
           'sampling a task,\n'
           'and let $R ( \\tau , q ) \\in \\{ 0 , 1 \\}$ be a task-dependent success indicator. We model the LLM agent '
           'as a policy π\n'
           'that induces a trajectory distribution. Without external skills, the agent generates trajectories by '
           'direct reasoning:\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\tau \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | q), \\quad q \\in \\mathcal {Q}.\\tag{2}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'To reduce redundant exploration and improve task completion, we equip the agent with a skills\n'
           'library $\\mathcal { D } =$ $\\{ s _ { 1 } , \\dotsc , s _ { | \\mathcal { D } | } \\}$ and a skill '
           'retriever that recalls a\n'
           'set of relevant skills for the current task. Concretely, given $q \\in \\mathcal { Q } .$ a retrieval '
           'function (typically\n'
           'implemented via semanticsimilarity retrieval) $\\rho : \\mathcal { Q } 2 ^ { \\mathcal { D } }$ . returns '
           'a skill\n'
           'subset $S _ { q } \\ = \\ \\rho ( q ) , S _ { q } \\ \\subseteq \\mathcal { D }$ . The LLM agent then '
           'generates a trajectory\n'
           'by conditioning on the retrieved skill set:\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\tau^ {\\prime} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | \\mathcal {S} _ {q}, q), \\quad q \\in \\mathcal {Q}.\\tag{3}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'Our objective is to design the skills library D and the usage within π such that the expected success rate '
           'is improved:\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\mathbb {E} _ {q \\in \\mathcal {Q}, \\tau^ {\\prime} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | \\mathcal {S} _ {q}, q)} R '
           '(\\tau^ {\\prime}, q) > \\mathbb {E} _ {q \\in \\mathcal {Q}, \\tau \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | q)} R (\\tau , '
           'q).\\tag{4}\n'
           '$$',
      chunk_index=3,

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:4:e6eed90fb5041166',
      text='## 3. SkillX Design and Implementation\n'
           '\n'
           '## 3.1. Multi-Level Skills Design\n'
           '\n'
           'In tool-centric agent scenarios, we structure the skills required by the model into three levels (see '
           'Figure 2):\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\mathcal {D} = S _ {\\text { plan }} \\oplus S _ {\\text { func }} \\oplus S _ {\\text { atomic '
           '}},\\tag{5}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'corresponding to planning skills, functional skills, and atomic skills, respectively. In a given\n'
           'environment $\\mathcal { E } ,$ let $\\tau$ denote the set of tool actions. (i) Atomic\n'
           'skill $s _ { \\mathrm { a t o m i c } }$ is aligned with a single tool $t \\in \\mathcal { T }$ and is '
           'modeled as an\n'
           'extended semantic specification of t, e.g., as enriched descriptions, constraints, or usage patterns that '
           'refine the\n'
           'effective behavior of t. (ii) Functional skill $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$ abstracts a subtask and can '
           'be regarded as\n'
           'a macro-operation that accomplishes a sub-query. We assume each task q admits a decomposition into n\n'
           'subtasks, $\\{ q _ { \\mathrm { s u b t a s k , 1 } } , q _ { \\mathrm { s u b t a s k , 2 } } , \\hdots , '
           'q _ { \\mathrm { s u b t a s k } , n } \\}$\n'
           'and each $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$ corresponds to skills to accomplish $q _ { \\mathrm { s u b t a s '
           'k } , i }$ .\n'
           'Specifically, $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$ is grounded in a set of tool actions, which can be '
           'instantiated as a\n'
           'composition of tools ${ \\mathcal { T } } _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } } \\subseteq { \\mathcal { T } }$ . '
           '(iii) planning\n'
           'skill $s _ { \\mathrm { p l a n } }$ aligns with the organizational structure of the subtasks (e.g., '
           'ordering,\n'
           'dependencies, and branching), specifying how functional skills should be composed to solve $q .$ Next, we '
           'describe the\n'
           'extraction methods for the three skill levels.',

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:5:480c5a3482429a55',
      text='## 3.2. Rollout and Skills Extraction\n'
           '\n'
           'Given a task $q ,$ we first perform m-sized rollouts, reusing the agent’s inference procedure to collect '
           'trajectories.\n'
           'We then extract the multi-level skills from these trajectories, with skill extractor $f .$ Details of the '
           'inference\n'
           'procedure are provided in Section 4.\n'
           '\n'
           'Planning Skills Extraction. Given a successful trajectory, we extract the planning skill $s _ { \\mathrm { '
           'p l a n } }$\n'
           'by compressing the trajectory into an ordered set of high-level steps. During this compression, we '
           'explicitly filter out\n'
           'non-essential transitions such as exploration, backtracking, and trial-and-error behaviors that are '
           'incidental to the\n'
           'final solution but detrimental to skill reuse. Moreover, for excessively long or verbose environment '
           'feedback, we apply\n'
           'summarization to obtain compact state descriptions, which improves the stability and fidelity of the '
           'extracted\n'
           'high-level skills.\n'
           '\n'
           'Functional Skills Extraction. We leverage the previously extracted planning skill $s _ { \\mathrm { p l a '
           'n } }$ to guide\n'
           'the extraction of functional skills. Concretely, given a plan and its corresponding trajectory, we '
           'iteratively prompt\n'
           'the model to extract the functional skill $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$ that aligns with the objective of '
           'each\n'
           'subtask $q _ { \\mathrm { s u b t a s k } , i }$ . Formally, each $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$ is '
           'represented with three\n'
           'key fields: name (the skill name), document (a description of inputs, outputs and usage notes), and '
           'content (the tool\n'
           'invocation pattern for completing subtask $q _ { \\mathrm { s u b t a s k } , i } )$\n'
           '\n'
           'Atomic Skills Extraction. Atomic skills are single tool specifications that extend the original tool '
           'schema with\n'
           'reusable, execution-oriented usage patterns. They serve as a low-level complement when higher-level '
           'functional\n'
           'skills $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$ are missing or incomplete. We prompt the model to\n'
           'distill $s _ { \\mathrm { a t o m i c } }$ from trajectories the invocation patterns, typical parameter '
           'configurations,\n'
           'and practical notes, especially constraints and common failure modes observed in real usage. The '
           'representation\n'
           'of $s _ { \\mathrm { a t o m i c } }$ is unified with $s _ { \\mathrm { f u n c } }$',
      chunk_index=5,

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:6:6d179dc76c46108d',
      text='## 3.3. Iterative Skills Refinement\n'
           '\n'
           'With only a limited amount of seed training data, a key question is whether we can maximize the utility of '
           'the available\n'
           'supervision to extract additional skills and continuously improve existing ones. Inspired by prior works '
           '(Cai et al.,\n'
           '2025b;a; Yuksekgonul et al., 2024), we adopt a text-based iterative optimization paradigm for the skill '
           'library.\n'
           'Concretely, at k-th iteration, we start from the current skill library $\\mathcal { D } ^ { ( k ) }$ , '
           'repeatedly\n'
           'rollouts from the training set, then extract multi-level skills. We subsequently apply a refinement '
           'operator $\\phi ,$\n'
           'including: Skills Merge and Skills Filter. Finally, we update the skill library $\\mathcal { D } ^ { ( k ) '
           '}$ with the\n'
           'refined skills to obtain skill library $\\mathcal { D } ^ { ( k + 1 ) }$ , including three update '
           'operations: add, modify\n'
           'or keep.\n'
           '\n'
           'Iterative Skills Library Construction. We construct the skill library in an iterative manner.\n'
           'Let $ { \\mathcal { D } ^ { ( 0 ) } } = \\emptyset$ be an initial empty library. In iteration $k = 0 , 1 , '
           '\\ldots ,$ we\n'
           'roll out the agent augmented with the current library $\\mathcal { D } ^ { ( k ) }$ on tasks sampled from '
           'the training\n'
           'set $\\mathcal { Q } _ { \\mathrm { t r a i n } }$ to obtain a set of trajectories\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\tau^ {(k)} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | \\rho_ {\\mathcal {D} ^ {(k)}} (q), q), \\quad q \\in \\mathcal {Q} _ '
           '{\\text {train}},\\tag{6}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'and denote $\\mathcal { K } ^ { ( k ) } = \\{ \\tau _ { 1 } ^ { ( k ) } , . . . , \\tau _ { N _ { k } } ^ '
           '{ ( k ) } \\}$ . A\n'
           'skill extractor $f$ produces a variable-size set of candidate skills from each\n'
           'trajectory, $S _ { i } ^ { ( k ) } = f ( \\tau _ { i } ^ { ( k ) } )$ and we aggregate all the skills '
           'extracted from the\n'
           'batch\n'
           'via $\\begin{array} { r } { S ^ { ( k ) } = \\bigcup _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { k } } S _ { i } ^ { ( k ) } } '
           '\\end{array}$ .\n'
           'Additionally, we define a refinement operator $\\phi$ to merge and filter the skills. The library is then '
           'updated as\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\mathcal {D} ^ {(k + 1)} \\triangleq \\mathcal {D} ^ {(k)} \\cup \\phi (\\mathcal {S} ^ {(k)}) = '
           '\\mathcal {D} ^ {(k)} \\cup \\phi \\left(\\bigcup_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {k}} \\mathcal {S} _ {i} ^ '
           '{(k)}\\right).\\tag{7}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'Let $\\mathcal { Q } _ { \\mathrm { t e s t } }$ denote a test distribution. We aim to iteratively improve '
           'the library\n'
           'such that the performance of the induced skill-conditioned agent is maximized\n'
           'on $\\mathcal { Q } _ { \\mathrm { t e s t } }$\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\max _ {k} \\mathbb {E} _ {q \\sim \\mathcal {Q} _ {\\mathrm{test}}} \\left[ \\mathbb {E} _ {\\tau \\sim '
           '\\pi (\\cdot | \\rho_ {\\mathcal {D} (k)} (q), q)} [ R (\\tau , q) ] \\right],\\tag{8}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'and we stop the iteration when this test performance no longer improves.\n'
           '\n'
           '![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-07-12/a6186265-7e30-41d9-8d19-d1b5e86aa6d7/eefd188023c6470340e492be2dd8047e37688e25a7240a7b22b4ac0ddd4dca75.jpg)\n'
           '\n'
           'Figure 2. SkillX provides an automated, iterative pipeline for constructing a skills library, integrating '
           'skills\n'
           'extraction. skills expansion and skills refinement. The skills library is organized into three levels: '
           'planning skills,\n'
           'functional skills, and atomic skills.\n'
           '\n'
           'Skills Merge. After extracting skills from each trajectory, we often obtain many functionally redundant '
           'skills that,\n'
           'despite surface differences, correspond to the same underlying skill pattern. How to update a single skill '
           'when multiple\n'
           'heterogeneous update directions are available? We merge skills from an optimization-based perspective. For '
           'a specific\n'
           'skill s with current embedding, we first retrieve and cluster a set of semantically similar skills using '
           'cosine\n'
           'similarity. The resulting cluster can be interpreted as providing multiple complementary update directions '
           'for the same\n'
           'underlying skill, a multi-dimensional refinement of s. Let $\\mathcal { Z } ( s ) = \\{ 1 , \\ldots , z '
           '\\}$ index the\n'
           'semantically similar skills associated with skill s. Each neighbor i induces a candidate update\n'
           'direction $\\delta _ { i } .$ , yielding a candidate updated state\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           's _ {i} ^ {\\prime} = s + \\delta_ {i}, \\quad i \\in \\mathcal {Z} (s).\\tag{9}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'We then aggregate these candidate directions into the final direction. The simplest form is to merge the\n'
           'directions: $\\begin{array} { r } { \\delta _ { \\mathrm { a g g } } ~ = ~ \\sum _ { i \\in \\mathcal { Z '
           '} ( s ) } \\delta _ { i } } \\end{array}$ .\n'
           'The final update is applied as\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           's ^ {+} = s + \\delta_ {\\mathrm{agg}}.\\tag{10}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'Specifically, we treat the semantically similar skills as multiple update views of the same skill, and we '
           'use the\n'
           'combined direction as the final update direction. Finally, we merge semantically similar skills into a '
           'single skill. If\n'
           'the merged skill becomes overly complex, we further decompose it into more modular, reusable skills.\n'
           '\n'
           'Skills Filter. We enforce skill quality via a strict two-stage filtering procedure. (1) General Filter. '
           'This stage\n'
           'removes skills that are unlikely to be portable or compositional, including those that depend on '
           'extraneous Python\n'
           'packages, expose overly idiosyncratic function-style definitions, or overly-encapsulated skills. (2) '
           'Tool-specific\n'
           'Filter. This stage mitigates tool-use hallucinations by validating each skill against the '
           'environment-provided tool\n'
           'schema, rejecting skills that reference non-existent tools, invalid parameters, or schema-incompatible '
           'argument\n'
           'structures. Together, these filters maintain a high-precision skill library while preserving flexibility '
           'across\n'
           'heterogeneous agent benchmarks.\n'
           '\n'
           'Skills Library Update. After completing Skill Merge and Skill Filter, we perform concrete updates to the '
           'skill\n'
           'library $\\mathcal { D } ^ { k }$ for the k-th iteration, including three types: add new skills, modify '
           'existing skills,\n'
           'and keep skills unchanged. Furthermore, the entire pipeline can be executed iteratively over multiple '
           'rounds. Through\n'
           'this continual update process, the skill library progressively improves in coverage, quality, and '
           'compositional\n'
           'richness, enabling increasingly effective skill reuse for downstream agent tasks.',
      chunk_index=6,

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:7:d7178601e997b80b',
      text='## 3.4. Exploratory Skills Expansion\n'
           '\n'
           'While skills distilled from a seed training set $\\mathcal { Q } _ { \\mathrm { t r a i n } }$ can already '
           'improve an\n'
           'agent’s performance, relying solely on scarce demonstrations is insufficient in complex environments with '
           'large tool\n'
           'spaces (e.g., (Trivedi et al., 2024) exposes hundreds of APIs). Inspired by Zhai et al. (2025), we adopt '
           'an Experience\n'
           'Guiding Exploration scheme to broaden coverage beyond what is observed in the seed data, encouraging the '
           'agent to\n'
           'interact with the environment and exercise a wider range of tools. We guide exploration using experience '
           'collected from\n'
           'rollouts on the seed set (e.g., tools the agent already uses reliably, tools with high failure rates, and '
           'tools that are\n'
           'never invoked), thereby prioritizing underexplored or failure-prone tools to improve sample efficiency. '
           'After collecting\n'
           'exploratory trajectories, we synthesize new tasks ${ \\mathcal { Q } } _ { \\mathrm { s y n } }$ from '
           'these interactions,\n'
           'and then rerun our skill acquisition and refinement pipeline on the resulting data to iteratively expand '
           'the skill\n'
           'library. Compared to the random exploration strategy (Zhai et al., 2025), our approach discovers a more '
           'diverse set of\n'
           'skills.',

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:8:5b800554266d8931',
      text='## 4. SkillX Usage\n'
           '\n'
           'Planning Skills Retrieval and Pseudo-Plan Rewriting. For a novel and complex agent task q, directly '
           'retrieving past\n'
           'experiences based solely on task similarity may lead to a mismatch between retrieved experiences and the '
           'actual\n'
           'execution trajectory. This issue becomes particularly pronounced in environments where execution dynamics '
           'are strongly\n'
           'influenced by user profiles, contextual constraints, or other external factors. To improve retrieval '
           'relevance, inspired\n'
           'by (Gao et al., 2022), we first retrieve high-level planning skills associated with similar\n'
           'tasks $\\mathcal { P } ( q ) = \\rho ( q )$ where ρ is a similarity retrieval function and $\\mathcal { P '
           '} ( q )$ is the\n'
           'retrieved planning skills. Then we prompt the model to self-rewrite a task-specific pseudo-plan '
           'conditioned on the\n'
           'current task $\\tilde { p } ( q ) = \\mathrm { L L M } _ { \\mathrm { r e w r i t e } } ( q , \\mathcal { '
           'P } ( q ) )$ .\n'
           'This rewritten pseudo-plan serves as an intermediate retrieval query to better align subsequent skill '
           'retrieval with the\n'
           'current execution setting. To mitigate hallucination risks and prevent speculative content from affecting '
           'agent\n'
           'behavior, the pseudo-plan is not injected into the final system prompt.\n'
           '\n'
           'Functional and Atomic Skills Retrieve. Given the rewritten\n'
           'pseudo-plan $\\tilde { p } ( q ) = \\{ \\mathrm { s t e p } _ { 1 } , \\mathrm { s t e p } _ { 2 } , '
           '\\ldots , \\mathrm { s t e p } _ { p } \\}$ ,\n'
           'we treat each step as a retrieval query to retrieve functional and atomic skills.\n'
           'For ${ \\mathrm { s t e p } } _ { i } ,$ we first retrieve relevant\n'
           'skills $S _ { i } ~ = ~ \\rho ( \\mathrm { s t e p } _ { i } )$ and then remove duplicates across\n'
           'steps, $S ^ { \\prime } ~ = ~ \\mathrm { d e d u p } \\Big ( \\bigcup _ { i = 1 } ^ { p } S _ { i } \\Big '
           ')$ . To keep the\n'
           'context concise and task-relevant, we further ask the LLM to self-filter the retrieved candidates and '
           'retain only\n'
           'applicable skills $ { S _ { q } } = \\mathrm { L L M }$ select $( q , \\tilde { p } ( q ) , S ^ { \\prime '
           '} )$ ,\n'
           'where $\\textstyle { \\mathcal { S } } _ { q }$ is the final skill set used for solving the query q.',

      source_spans=(SourceSpan(start_offset=21693, end_offset=21712),

           '## 5.1. Experimental Settings\n'
           '\n'
           'Benchmarks and Metrics. We conduct the evaluation on complex, long-horizon, user-interactive agent '
           'benchmarks,\n'
           'including $\\mathrm { \\ B F C L - v 3 }$ (Patil et al., 2025), AppWorld (Trivedi et al., 2024), and '
           '$\\tau ^ { 2 }$\n'
           '-bench (Barres et al., 2025). For BFCL-$\\mathbf { v } 3 ,$ , we use the base multi-turn category and '
           'randomly split it\n'
           'into 50 training instances and 150 test instances. AppWorld provides 90 training instances and the Test '
           'Normal category\n'
           'as test set. $\\tau ^ { 2 } .$ -bench defines training and test splits for each sub-domain. Additional '
           'details are\n'
           'provided in the Appendix A.1. For AppWorld and BFCL-v3, we report Avg@4 and Pass@4, the average success '
           'rate over four\n'
           'independent runs and the probability of succeeding at least once across four runs, respectively. Following '
           'the (Barres\n'
           'et al., 2025) evaluation setup, we report Passˆ1, the pass rate over running four times.\n'
           '\n'
           'Models and Baselines. To assess the effectiveness of SkillX, we evaluate three Agentic base models that '
           'vary in model\n'
           'size and reasoning style (thinking and nonthinking), including Qwen3-32B (Yang et al., 2025), '
           'Kimi-K2-Instruct-0905 (\n'
           'Team et al., 2025b), and GLM-4.6 (Team et al., 2025a). Among them, GLM-4.6 has been reported to exhibit '
           'strong native\n'
           'agentic capabilities in agent midtraining, serving as a competitive backbone for our study.\n'
           '\n'
           'We compare against four representative baselines: (1) Nomemory, which performs inference without '
           'retrieving any prior\n'
           'experience; (2) A-Mem (Xu et al., 2025), a system that dynamically manages structured episodic memories; '
           '(3) AWM (Wang\n'
           'et al., 2025c), which reuses modular workflows distilled from historical trajectories; and (4) ExpeL (Zhao '
           'et al.,\n'
           '2024), which retrieves relevant past trajectories as few-shot demonstrations and incorporates distilled '
           'insights to\n'
           'improve LLM performance. For a fair comparison, all methods retrieve experience only based on the user’s '
           'initial query\n'
           'and insert the retrieved content into the system prompt following a unified protocol. Full baseline '
           'details are provided\n'
           'in the Appendix A.2.\n'
           '\n'
           'Implementation Details. To construct SkillX, we use GLM-4.6 (Team et al., 2025a) independently rollouts '
           'four times per\n'
           'training task, followed by skill extraction, skill refinement, and skill expansion. The maximum number of '
           'refinement\n'
           'iterations is set to 3. For efficiency, we limit environment exploration to one rollout per training task; '
           'the sampling\n'
           'temperature is 1.0 during exploration. We use Qwen3-Embedding-8B (Zhang et al., 2025d) for both skill '
           'deduplication and\n'
           'skill retrieval, with a minimum cosine similarity threshold of 0.45 for retrieval. During solving new '
           'tasks, we use the\n'

           'settings: (1)\n'
           'Distillation paradigm: a strong agent (GLM-4.6) is used to extract experiences to build an experience '
           'repository, and\n'

           'is kept\n'
           'consistent to the execution model to enable self-extraction, following the original experimenta protocol '
           'of each method.\n'

      text='## 5.2. Main Results\n'
           '\n'
           'SkillX Boost Agentic Performance of Base LLMs. As shown in Table 1, SkillX improves the base model’s '
           'performance. In\n'
           'particular, Qwen3-32B gains roughly around 10 points across multiple benchmarks. For K2 (Kimi-K2- '
           'Instruct-0905), we\n'
           'observe a clear improvement on App-World, whereas the gains are modest on the other two tool call '
           'intensive benchmarks.\n'
           'We infer this is because K2 relies more heavily on the original tool schema and does not effectively '
           'leverage the\n'
           'additional contextual information.\n'
           '\n'
           'Multi-Level Skills Design Outperform Other Forms of Experience Representation. When the experience '
           'extraction model is\n'
           'aligned with the execution model, SkillX consistently outperforms all baseline methods, as indicated by '
           'the methods\n'
           'with ∗ in Table 1. Among them, ExpeL retrieves past trajectories and uses them as few-shot demonstrations, '
           'which\n'
           'provides a more direct performance gain than the other baselines. However, the agent capabil-\n'
           '\n'
           'ity required for multi-level skill decoupling offers a more advantageous form of experience '
           'representation.\n'
           '\n'
           'Suboptimal Experience Representations Hinder Transfer Performance. We further evaluate the GLM-4.6 '
           'extracted experience\n'
           'with AWM and ExpeL on the weaker models, see the results of methods with ‡ in Table 1. However, the '
           'performance still\n'
           'lagged behind that of SkillX. This indicates that distilling experience from a strong model is effective, '
           'but the form\n'
           'of experience representation is even more critical. Consequently, suboptimal experience representation can '
           'hinder\n'
           'effective experience transfer. These results further demonstrate the advantage of SkillX in transferring '
           'experience\n'
           'across base models.\n'
           '\n'
           'SkillX can Expand Base Model’s Capability Boundary. We observe that experience-based learning leads to '
           'substantial\n'
           'Pass@4 improvements for the weaker models, K2 and Qwen3-32B. This suggests that, in practice, the most '
           'direct way to\n'
           'extend the capability boundary of a base model is to distill knowledge from a stronger model (Yue et al., '
           '2025). In\n'
           'contrast, for the stronger model GLM-4.6, neither the baseline nor SkillX yields a significant gain in '
           'Pass@4. This\n'
           'indicates that stronger models already possess robust capabilities in exploration, planning, and tool use, '
           'leaving\n'

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:11:96e0b65784be8fc2',
      text='## 5.3. Analysis\n'
           '\n'
           'Which skill is more effective? We analyze the behaviors of our multi-level skill across models on '
           'AppWorld, and the\n'
           'results are shown in Figure 3 (a) and Figure 3 (b). (i) Planning skills consistently reduce the number of '
           'execution\n'
           'steps across all models, with particularly pronounced gains for weaker models such as Qwen3-32B and K2, '
           'especially when\n'
           'combined with Functional Skills. We attribute this to their limited exploration capability in complex '
           'environments.\n'
           'Notably, for Qwen3-32B, adding Functional and Atomic Skills can even hurt performance, as the model tends '
           'to\n'
           'over-imitate retrieved skills rather than adapt them to novel tasks. For stronger models, pseudo-planning '
           'may fail to\n'
           'faithfully capture underlying environment dynamics in complex scenarios, and can therefore become '
           'counterproductive. (\n'
           'ii) Functional skills contribute the most to overall performance improvements: equipping K2 and GLM-4.6 '
           'with Functional\n'
           'and Atomic Skills alone already yields observable gains, highlighting the advantage of skills as an '
           'effective\n'
           'representation of experience. (iii) Atomic skills provide crucial clarifications for key APIs. When they '
           'are absent,\n'
           'performance drops substantially, further validating the need to supplement tool schemas and to cover tools '
           'missing from\n'
           'Functional Skills. Finally, we find that GLM-4.6 benefits the most from using all skill types; K2 performs '
           'best with\n'
           'Functional + Atomic Skills; and Qwen3-32B achieves its best performance when only Planning Skills are '
           'enabled. This\n'
           'further demonstrates that multi-level skills can comprehensively cover the capabilities required for '
           'diverse models to\n'
           'execute agent tasks.\n'
           '\n'
           'Iterative Refinement Strategies Further Enhances SkillX Performance. We evaluate effectiveness of '
           'multi-round iterative\n'
           'refinement for the skill library of SkillX on AppWorld (Figure 3 (c)). Overall, multiple iterations '
           'further improve\n'
           'performance on both training and test sets. Leveraging existing training data, the process continually '
           'improves various\n'
           'aspects of skills, such as documentation and content. Besides, it can slightly expand the size of the '
           'skill library (\n'
           'Figure 3 (d)). However, when training data are limited, text-only optimization can lead to overfitting. '
           'Thus, selecting\n'
           'an appropriate number of update rounds is crucial to obtain a higher-quality skill library.\n'
           '\n'
           'Skill Expansion Strategies Improve Generalization. We compare two skill expansion strategies: random '
           'exploration and\n'
           'experience-guided expansion. The results are as shown in Figure 3 (d). In terms of skill growth, the '
           'experience-guided\n'
           'strategy yields substantially more novel skills, as random exploration treats past executions in isolation '
           'and\n'
           'repeatedly rediscovers already identified skills. Empirically, the experience guided strategy yields '
           'performance\n'
           'improvement through skill expansion. Overall, our results indicate that in complex environments, '
           'particularly under\n'
           'scarce training data, skill expansion is a crucial component of experience learning.\n'
           '\n'
           'SkillX Enhances Agent Execution Efficiency. Learning from experience not only improves the performance of '
           'the base\n'
           'model, but also enhances the execution efficiency of the agent. Our experiments further corroborate this '
           'effect (see\n'
           'Figure 3 (e) and Figure 3 (f)). Although we do not achieve the minimum number of execution steps or the '
           'fewest input\n'
           'tokens, we obtain the best overall performance (see Table 1). These results further highlight the '
           'advantages of our\n'
           'multi-level skill design and skills library construction.',

      text='## 6.2. Ablation Study on Three Components of SkillX\n'
           '\n'
           'We conduct ablation studies on the three key components of SkillX, i.e., multi-level skills design, skills '
           'refinement,\n'
           'and skills expansion, as shown in Table 3. The results in Table 3 suggest that SkillX is robust to its '
           'underlying\n'
           'experience representation, while iterative refinement and skill expansion can offer further improvements '
           'depending on\n'
           'the model and the particular combination of components.\n'
           '\n'
           'Please note that we do not perform ablations of skills iteration and skills expansion on τ '
           '<sup>2</sup>-Bench. This is\n'
           'because $\\tau ^ { 2 } \\mathrm { . }$ Bench is a user-interactive benchmark whose tool schemas are '
           'relatively simple in\n'
           'both number and dependency structure, and its training set already covers many task patterns directly. '
           'More broadly, for\n'
           'user-centric benchmarks of this type (e.g., dialogue benchmarks), it remains an open question whether '
           'experience\n'
           'learning centered around toolschema-based skills is the most appropriate formulation. Therefore, we '
           'believe that\n'
           'component studies on skill iteration and skill expansion are less suitable for $\\tau ^ { 2 } .$ -Bench, '
           'and we do not\n'
           'include them in our ablation experiments.\n'

CHUNK 15 BEGIN
Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:15:109553a3bc4fffb3',
      text='## 7. Related Work\n'
           '\n'
           'Encoding For Agent Experience. With the advent of the experience era (Sutton, 2025), agents can achieve '
           'selfevolving (\n'
           'Gao et al., 2025; Fang et al., 2025b; Xia et al., 2026) by encoding past experience and reusing it in '
           'context (Dou et\n'
           'al., 2026) to guide future behavior. Existing approaches to text token-level experience encoding (Zhang et '
           'al., 2025b;\n'
           'Hu et al., 2025) can be broadly grouped into three categories: (i) Case-based Experience: Agents directly '
           'store\n'
           'successful task-execution trajectories and retrieve them later as few-shot examples to new problem solving '
           '(Zhao et al.,\n'
           '2024; Zheng et al., 2024; Zhou et al., 2025). (ii) Strategy-based Experience: By summarizing and '
           'contrasting successful\n'
           'versus failed trajectories, agents distill higherlevel insights or workflows (Cao et al., 2025; Ouyang et '
           'al., 2025; Cai\n'
           'et al., 2025a; Wang et al., 2025c; Tang et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2025a). (iii) Skill-based Experience: '
           'Trajectories\n'
           'are segmented and distilled into modular, reusable skills, such as textual skills or programmatic skills '
           '(Wang et al.,\n'
           '2025b;a; 2024; Fang et al., 2025c; Han et al., 2025;\n'
           '\n'
           'Chen et al., 2026; Zheng et al., 2026; Wang et al., 2026a; Zhou et al., 2026a; Zhang et al., 2026b; Ni et '
           'al., 2026;\n'
           'Zhou et al., 2026b). However, it remains unclear which unified experience representation is both easily '
           'pluggable and\n'
           'consistently effective, especially in diverse and complex agentic tool-use scenarios (Trivedi et al., '
           '2024; Yao et al.,\n'
           '2024; Patil et al., 2025; Barres et al., 2025; He et al., 2025; Li et al., 2025; Zheng et al., 2025; Jiang '
           'et al., 2026;\n'
           'Xing et al., 2026; Li, 2026; Li et al., 2026). In this work, we adopt a hybrid representation, high-level '
           'planning\n'
           'coupled with textual skills, which yields substantial improvements for the base model.\n'
           '\n'
           'Agent Experience Knowledge Base Construction. The construction pipeline of an experience knowledge base '
           'typically\n'
           'consists of two steps: static construction and dynamic updating. (i) Static construction repeatedly '
           'attempts tasks on a\n'
           'training set or human-curated information sources, extracts experience, and iteratively refines it until '
           'performance\n'
           'plateaus (Zhang et al., 2025c; Cai et al., 2025b; Anthropic, 2025; Wang et al., 2026b; Gallego, 2026; Yang '
           'et al.,\n'
           '2026a). (ii) Dynamic updating updates the ExperienceKB immediately after executing new tasks, enabling '
           'experience reuse\n'
           'in subsequent tasks (Latimer et al., 2025; Fang et al., 2025a; Cao et al., 2025; Du et al., 2025; Yang et '
           'al., 2026b;\n'
           'Yao et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2026a; Liang et al., 2026).\n'
           '\n'
           'While dynamic updating is central to continual learning from experience, pre-building a strong static '
           'ExperienceKB\n'
           'remains necessary in practice. However, under the taskscarcity challenge in complex agent settings (Patil '
           'et al., 2025;\n'
           'Barres et al., 2025; He et al., 2025; Li et al., 2025), we further extend skills by combining task '
           'synthesis (Zha et\n'
           'al., 2025; Mai et al., 2025; Shi et al., 2025; Ramrakhya et al., 2025; Guo et al., 2025) to construct more '
           'challenging\n'
           'tasks. To our knowledge, this is the first work to provide a directly reusable skill knowledge base '
           'together with an\n'
           'automated pipeline for skill construction.',
      chunk_index=15,
      start_offset=42462,
      end_offset=45679,
      source_spans=(SourceSpan(start_offset=42462, end_offset=42481),

CHUNK 16 BEGIN
Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:16:8aaf1347936a34e2',
      text='## 8. Conclusion\n'
           '\n'
           'We introduced SkillX, an automated framework for building a plug-and-play skill library for LLM-based '
           'agents. To enable\n'
           'more efficient experience transfer, we design a multi-level skills, including planning skills, functional '
           'skills, and\n'
           'atomic skills from the perspective of tool granularity. SkillX iteratively refines and expands the library '
           'through three\n'
           'core components: i) skills extraction, which rolls out an agent with the current library and extracts '
           'multi-level\n'
           'skills; ii) skills refinement, which iteratively improves skills using execution feedback, while '
           'maintaining quality via\n'
           'skill merging and strict filtering; and iii) exploratory skills expansion, which proactively broadens '
           'coverage beyond\n'
           'the seed training set. Our experiments demonstrate that SkillX transfers effectively to other models and '
           'provides\n'
           'advantages in experience representation. Finally, we will release the optimized skill library constructed '
           'by SkillX to\n'
           'facilitate further community exploration.',
      chunk_index=16,
      start_offset=45680,
      end_offset=46685,

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:17:4c7e90164fa75050',
      text='## Impact Statements\n'
           '\n'
           'This work advances generalizable agent learning by transforming isolated trial-and-error experience into a '
           'reusable,\n'
           'structured skill knowledge base that can be shared across agents and environments. By enabling weaker '
           'agents to benefit\n'
           'from skills distilled by stronger ones, the proposed framework reduces redundant exploration, improves '
           'sample\n'
           'efficiency, and lowers the computational and environmental costs of training LLM agents. The plug-and-play '
           'design\n'
           'promotes modularity and reproducibility, supporting broader adoption in long-horizon, user-interactive '
           'applications.\n'
           'Potential risks include over-reliance on pre-built skills and the propagation of biases present in source '
           'agents;\n'
           'however, the automated refinement and expansion mechanisms provide a pathway to mitigate stagnation and '
           'encourage\n'
           'continual adaptation.',
      chunk_index=17,
      start_offset=46686,

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:18:e37cce471bdf3b52',
      text='## Limitations\n'
           '\n'
           'Cross-environment transfer. SkillX is currently most naturally applicable when skills can be grounded in a '
           'relatively\n'
           'stable tool environment. The extracted skills are associated with specific tool schemas, which makes '
           'direct reuse across\n'
           'substantially different domains or tool ecosystems less straightforward.\n'
           '\n'
           'User-interactive settings. The current study focuses mainly on tool-using agent environments. More user '
           'interactive\n'
           'scenarios, particularly dialogue scenarios without function calls, are not yet the primary focus of this '
           'work.',

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:24:fd6169999f64447b',
      text='## A. Detailed Experiments Settings\n'
           '\n'
           '## A.1. Benchmark Details\n'
           '\n'
           'BFCL-v3 Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard V3 (BFCL-v3) (Patil et al., 2025) is a benchmark for '
           'evaluating function\n'
           'calling and tool use in large language models. It emphasizes multi-turn interaction and multi-step '
           'reasoning. The\n'
           'benchmark contains over 1,800 test instances and supports multiple programming languages, including '
           'Python, Java, and\n'
           'JavaScript. Models are required to generate valid API calls and handle non-trivial interaction patterns. '
           'Evaluation\n'
           'considers both structural validity and functional correctness. We first check whether the generated code '
           'is\n'
           'syntactically valid using Abstract Syntax Tree analysis, and then execute it to verify that the outputs '
           'match the\n'
           'expected results. A task is considered successful only when the agent produces all required function calls '
           'with correct\n'
           'syntax and returns the correct computationa outcomes. In this work, we report Avg@4, which measures the '
           'average task\n'
           'success rate across four independent trials, and Pass@4, which measures the probability that at least one '
           'of the four\n'
           'trials succeeds.\n'
           '\n'
           'Appworld AppWorld (Trivedi et al., 2024) is a benchmark suite for evaluating function calling agents and '
           'interactive\n'
           'coding systems in realistic application environments. It simulates an ecosystem of nine widely used '
           'applications, such\n'
           'as email services, music streaming platforms, and payment systems, and provides 457 API endpoints together '
           'with activity\n'
           'data from around 100 virtual users. Tasks in AppWorld are typically long-horizon and require executing '
           'extended\n'
           'sequences of interdependent actions. Many tasks involve discovering appropriate APIs rather than directly '
           'reusing\n'
           'familiar patterns, which places additional demands on exploration and planning. The benchmark also '
           'exhibits a noticeable\n'
           'distribution gap between training and test sets, where API usage patterns and task structures in the test '
           'set differ\n'
           'from those observed during training. In addition, task execution is tightly coupled with the evolving '
           'environment state.\n'
           'Intermediate actions modify the system state and influence future decisions, which increases sensitivity '
           'to planning\n'
           'errors and makes robust multi-step reasoning more difficult. Evaluation is based on state-driven unit '
           'tests that assess\n'
           'task completion from multiple aspects. AppWorld provides both task-level and scenario-level metrics. In '
           'this work, we\n'
           'use Task Goal Completion as the primary measure of performance. Following the standard protocol, we report '
           'Avg@4 and\n'
           'Pass@4 across four independent trials.\n'
           '\n'
           'τ <sup>2</sup>-Bench τ <sup>2</sup>-Bench (Barres et al., 2025) evaluates tool use in conversational agent '
           'settings,\n'
           'with a strong emphasis on user-agent interaction. The benchmark simulates multi-turn dialogues between a '
           'user and an\n'
           'agent, aiming to reflect realistic conversational behavior. Agents must track dialogue context across '
           'turns, interpret\n'
           'user requests, select and invoke APIs appropriately, and follow domain-specific business rules. The tasks '
           'cover domains\n'
           'such as airline customer service and retail customer service. The interactive nature of the benchmark '
           'requires agents to\n'
           'respond to user feedback, maintain coherent dialogue flow, and coordinate tool use with the ongoing '
           'conversation.\n'
           'Performance is assessed based on task completion accuracy, correctness of tool use, and compliance with '
           'policies. In\n'
           'this work, we conduct four independent trials per task and report Pass@1 on each of the three domains.',

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:25:b89f63ddd40734da',
      text='## A.2. Baseline Details\n'
           '\n'
           'A-Mem A-Mem (Xu et al., 2025) is an agentic memory framework that equips LLM-based agents with the ability '
           'to maintain\n'
           'and utilize long-term knowledge over extended interactions. The method organizes accumulated experiences '
           'into a\n'
           'memory-centric structure, enabling agents to selectively retain, retrieve, and revise stored information '
           'according to\n'
           'task objectives and observed outcomes. Rather than treating memory as a passive log, A-Mem emphasizes '
           'autonomous memory\n'
           'management driven by the agent’s goals and interaction context. In our experiments, we reproduce A-Mem '
           'based on its\n'
           'publicly available implementation, with minor prompt adaptations to support memory writing and '
           'organization during task\n'
           'interactions.\n'
           '\n'
           'AWM AWM (Agent Workflow Memory) (Wang et al., 2025c) is a memory-augmented agent framework that focuses on '
           'discovering\n'
           'reusable workflow patterns from past task executions. The method stores completed task trajectories as '
           'episodic\n'
           'experiences and derives higher-level procedural knowledge by analyzing multiple successful examples. '
           'Experience\n'
           'retrieval follows a lightweight lexical matching strategy. Textual representations of task queries and '
           'stored\n'
           'experiences are mapped to sparse term-based vectors, and relevance is measured using cosine similarity. A '
           'small set of\n'
           'highly relevant experiences is selected for downstream analysis, with subsampling applied when multiple '
           'candidates\n'
           'exhibit comparable similarity. Workflow induction is performed by prompting a language model to analyze '
           'the retrieved\n'
           'successful trajectories and summarize recurring action patterns. Rather than relying on explicit symbolic '
           'rules or\n'
           'predefined workflow schemas, AWM captures reusable procedural structures directly from empirical task '
           'executions.\n'
           'Retrieved experiences are incorporated as conversational message objects (e.g., HumanMessage and '
           'AIMessage), enabling\n'
           'the language model to process exemplar interactions naturally within the dialogue context.\n'
           '\n'
           'ExpeL ExpeL (Zhao et al., 2024) is an experience-driven learning framework that improves agent performance '
           'by reflecting\n'
           'on past successes and failures. The method stores task execution trajectories and generates experiential '
           'knowledge by\n'
           'contrasting successful and unsuccessful outcomes for the same task. In our experiments, we reproduce ExpeL '
           'by collecting\n'
           'both successful trajectories (reward ≥ 1.0) and failed trajectories (reward < 1.0). For each successful '
           'example, a small\n'
           'number of failed trajectories from the same task type are selected for comparative analysis. A large '
           'language model is\n'
           'prompted to analyze the paired trajectories and generate natural-language critiques that highlight key '
           'decision\n'
           'differences and improvement suggestions. These critiques are retained as unstructured textual experiences '
           'and reused as\n'
           'guidance in subsequent tasks.',

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:26:c5b09ef33aeadd1d',
      text='## A.3. Implementation Details\n'
           '\n'
           'Skills Extraction. During the experience extraction stage, which comprises both reasoning and experience '
           'extraction, we\n'
           'employ GLM-4.6 with a temperature of 0.9. For each task in the training set, we independently sample four '
           'trajectories.\n'
           'Environment feedback exceeding 1500 tokens is summarized. We cluster the extracted skills using DBSCAN '
           '(Density-Based\n'
           'Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise) with a cosine similarity threshold of 0.9. For each '
           'cluster, we truncate\n'
           'the skill set to at most 15 skills. Skill updates are performed with up to three iterative refinement '
           'rounds. During the\n'
           'skill expansion stage, we set the exploration model temperature to 1.0 and perform 1 time to explore '
           'environment for\n'
           'each training task.\n'
           '\n'
           'Skills Usage. We build a skill semantic vector store using FAISS with an HNSW index under cosine '
           'similarity (via\n'
           'L2-normalized embeddings and inner-product search). At query time, we first perform a broad retrieval of '
           'the Top-100\n'
           'nearest skills. Candidates are then filtered by a hybrid relevance threshold: we keep only results whose '
           'cosine\n'
           'similarity is at least 0.45, and also within 0.08 of the best match for that query, ensuring both a '
           'minimum quality\n'
           'floor and adaptive selectivity. To reduce near-duplicate skills, we apply semantic deduplication by '
           'removing items whose\n'
           'pairwise cosine similarity exceeds 0.95, retaining the higher-scoring representative. Finally, we return '
           'up to 8 skills\n'
           'after applying Maximal Marginal Relevance (MMR) for diversity-aware selection, using a relevance–diversity '
           'trade-off\n'
           'weight of 0.75 to emphasize relevance while mitigating redundancy.',
      chunk_index=26,
      start_offset=77377,
      end_offset=79029,
      source_spans=(SourceSpan(start_offset=77377, end_offset=77408),
                    SourceSpan(start_offset=77409, end_offset=78145),
                    SourceSpan(start_offset=78146, end_offset=79029)),
      start_block=211,
      end_block=213,
      content_hash='c5b09ef33aeadd1dc827bd11d70b7fbd963726d730fa6c50a9341c61ec219163',
      metadata={'block_kinds': (<BlockKind.HEADING: 'heading'>,
                                <BlockKind.PARAGRAPH: 'paragraph'>,
                                <BlockKind.PARAGRAPH: 'paragraph'>),
                'section_paths': (('SkillX: Automatically Constructing Skill Knowledge Bases for Agents',

           '\n'
           '• Skill is a dictionary with four keys: name, document, content and tools.\n'
           '\n'
           '1. name: the skill’s name.\n'
           '\n'
           '2. document: the skill’s functionality, the key parameters, the final output of the skill, and any '
           'important notes.\n'
           '\n'
           '3. content: the concrete implementation of the skill.\n'
           '\n'
           '4. tools: the key tools used in the skill (list).\n'
           '\n'
           '\n'
           '• The skill is abstract, modular, and reusable. Specifically, the skill name must be generic under one '
           'application (\n'
           'e.g., {good example} instead of {bad example}. The skill must use parameters instead of hard-coded values '
           '(e.g.,\n'
           'specific email address {email address}). The skill body must be self-contained.\n'
           '\n'
           '• Explicitly declare the key parameters and the final output data types using type hints. Example: '
           'Parameters: param:\n'
           'str; Outputs: output: list[dict]:\n'

           '\n'

      text='# SkillX: Automatically Constructing Skill Knowledge Bases for Agents\n'
           '\n'
           'Chenxi Wang <sup>*</sup> <sup>1</sup> <sup>2</sup> Zhuoyun Yu <sup>*</sup> <sup>1</sup> <sup>2</sup> Xin '
           'Xie <sup>\n'
           '2</sup> Wuguannan Yao <sup>2</sup> Runnan Fang <sup>1</sup> Shuofei Qiao <sup>1</sup> Kexin Cao '

           '\n'
           '## Abstract\n'
           '\n'
           'Learning from experience is critical for building capable large language model (LLM) agents, yet '
           'prevailing\n'
           'self-evolving paradigms remain inefficient: agents learn in isolation, repeatedly rediscover similar '
           'behaviors from\n'
           'limited experience, resulting in redundant exploration and poor generalization. To address this problem, '
           'we propose\n'
           'SkillX, a fully automated framework for constructing a plug-and-play skill knowledge base that can be '
           'reused across\n'
           'agents and environments. SkillX operates through a fully automated pipeline built on three synergistic '
           'innovations: (i)\n'
           'Multi-Level Skills Design, which distills raw trajectories into three-tiered hierarchy of strategic plans, '
           'functional\n'
           'skills, and atomic skills; (ii) Iterative Skills Refinement, which automatically revises skills based on '
           'execution\n'
           'feedback to continuously improve library quality; and (iii) Exploratory Skills Expansion, which '
           'proactively generates\n'
           'and validates novel skills to expand coverage beyond seed training data. Using a strong backbone agent '
           '(GLM-4.6), we\n'
           'automatically build a reusable skill library and evaluate its transferability on challenging '
           'long-horizon,\n'
           'user-interactive benchmarks, including AppWorld, BFCL-v3, and τ<sup>2</sup>-Bench. Experiments show that '
           'SkillKB\n'
           'consistently improves task success and execution efficiency when plugged into weaker base agents, '
           'highlighting the\n'
           'importance of structured, hierarchical experi ence representations for generalizable agent learning. Our '
           'code will be\n'
           'publicly available soon at https://github.com/zjunlp/SkillX.',

Chunk(chunk_id='chunk:1:c5591057856c759b',
      text='## 1. Introduction\n'
           '\n'
           'Large language model (LLM) based agents (OpenAI, 2025; DeepSeek-AI, 2025; Team et al., 2025b; Yang et al., '
           '2025)\n'
           '\n'
           '![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-07-12/a6186265-7e30-41d9-8d19-d1b5e86aa6d7/7b12c89605093f39cb3c9c20c1a4d57db51995f410c7631bdd76507dd334ab8a.jpg)\n'
           '\n'
           'Figure 1. Claude Skills follow a long-context, progressively disclosed format, which requires a complex '
           'sandboxing\n'
           'system and multiple interactions, thereby posing challenges to robust reasoning. In contrast, SkillX '
           'adopts a\n'
           'hierarchical, itemized representation that can be stored and retrieved via a lightweight retrieval module '
           'and injected\n'
           'into the system prompt in one time, making it easier to transfer across base models.\n'
           '\n'
           'have recently demonstrated remarkable progress in longhorizon decision making with tools, enabling complex '
           'behaviors\n'
           'such as API calling (Trivedi et al., 2024; Patil et al., 2025; Li et al., 2025), web navigation (Yao et '
           'al., 2023; Zhou\n'
           'et al., 2024; Mialon et al., 2023), scientific discovery (Ou et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025; Qiao et al., '
           '2025; Novikov\n'
           'et al., 2025), and interactive assistants (Barres et al., 2025; Yao et al., 2024; He et al., 2025). '
           'Despite these\n'
           'advances, most agents still approach each new task largely from scratch, relying on direct reasoning or '
           'limited\n'
           'taskspecific demonstrations. This paradigm is costly, brittle, and fundamentally at odds with how '
           'intelligent systems\n'
           'are expected to accumulate and reuse experience over time.\n'
           '\n'
           'A natural resolution is to enable agents to learn from experience (Sutton, 2025). Recent work has explored '
           'selfevolving\n'
           'agents that iteratively reflect on past executions and improve their behavior over time (Wang et al., '
           '2025c; Fang et\n'
           'al., 2025c; Zhao et al., 2024; Xu et al., 2025; Cao et al., 2025). While promising, these approaches often '
           'fail to\n'
           'deliver scalable and transferable gains. In practice, experience learning typically suffers from three '
           'structural\n'
           'limitations. (1) Isolated Learning: agents execute the same tasks repeatedly and re-extract similar '
           'experiences\n'
           'independently, leading to substantial redundancy. (2) Weak Generalization of Experience: in complex '
           'environments,\n'
           'high-quality training data are scarce, so the mined experiences often transfer poorly to new tasks. (3) '
           'Model Capability\n'
           'Bottleneck: when experience is harvested solely through an agent’s own exploration and reflection, what '
           'can be extracted\n'
           'is ultimately capped by the agent’s current capability frontier. These challenges point to a more '
           'fundamental question:\n'
           'What form of experience can be broadly reusable across agents of varying capabilities and across diverse '
           'environments?\n'
           '\n'
           'Existing work has proposed multiple representations of experience, such as insights (Cao et al., 2025; '
           'Ouyang et al.,\n'
           '2025), workflows (Wang et al., 2025c;b; Han et al., 2025), or trajectories (Zhao et al., 2024; Fang et '
           'al., 2025c).\n'
           'However, none of these representations simultaneously offer strong transferability, efficient retrieval, '
           'and direct\n'
           'executability. Inspired by Claude Skills (Anthropic, 2025), we argue that skills provide a more suitable '
           'abstraction:\n'
           'they encapsulate reusable competencies that directly support task execution. Nonetheless, prior '
           'skill-based designs\n'
           'often rely on longcontext, progressive disclosure, which place heavy demands on reasoning and environment\n'
           'instrumentation, limiting robustness and practical reuse, as illustrated in Figure 1.\n'
           '\n'
           'In this work, we introduce SkillX, a fully automated framework for constructing a plug-and-play skill '
           'knowledge base\n'
           'from agent experience. Our core insight is that transferable experience should be organized '
           'hierarchically, rather than\n'
           'as monolithic behaviors. SkillX therefore represents experience at three complementary levels: (i) '
           'Planning Skills,\n'
           'which capture high-level task organization; (ii) Functional Skills, which implement reusable, toolbased '
           'subroutines;\n'
           'and (iii) Atomic Skills, which encode execution-oriented usage patterns and constraints. This multi-level '
           'design yields\n'
           'skills that are concise, composable, and robust to distributional shifts. SkillX builds such a skill '
           'library through a\n'
           'fully automated pipeline. A strong backbone agent first performs rollouts on training tasks and distills '
           'multi-level\n'
           'skills from successful trajectories. The extracted skills are then iteratively refined through '
           'consolidation and\n'
           'validation, improving library quality over time. Finally, SkillX performs experience-guided exploration to '
           'proactively\n'
           'expand the skill space by targeting under-utilized tools and failure-prone behaviors, enabling '
           'generalization beyond the\n'
           'initial training distribution.\n'
           '\n'
           'To build a reliable, plug-and-play skill library, we instantiate SkillX with a strong agent backbone, '
           'GLM-4.6 (Team et\n'
           'al., 2025a), and pre-build a skill library on challenging, user-interactive, long-horizon benchmarks, '
           'including:\n'
           'AppWorld (Trivedi et al., 2024), BFCL-v3 (Patil et al., 2025), and $\\tau ^ { 2 }$ -Bench (Barres et al., '
           '2025). Our\n'
           'experiments show that this plug-and-play skill library can be directly plugged into base agents (e.g., '
           'Qwen3-32B (Yang\n'
           'et al., 2025)), yielding around a 10% performance improvement while also improving execution efficiency. '
           'We further\n'
           'demonstrate the advantages of our multi-level skill design for experience representation, and show that '
           'both iterative\n'
           'refinement and skill expansion provide additional gains. In a nutshell, we conclude our contributions as:\n'
           '\n'
           '• We propose a hierarchical skill representation that transforms raw trajectories into reusable planning, '
           'functional,\n'
           'and atomic skills.\n'
           '\n'
           '• We present SkillX, a fully automated and extensible framework for pre-building plug-and-play skill '
           'libraries for LLM\n'
           'agents, featuring iterative refinement and skill expansion.\n'
           '\n'
           '• We release the resulting plug-and-play skill library and provide strong empirical evidence across '
           'multiple agent\n'
           'benchmarks that it can directly enhance the capabilities of weaker agents.\n'
           '\n'
           '## 2. Preliminaries\n'
           '\n'
           'Agent Definition We consider a general interactive setting where an agent solves tasks by acting in an '
           'environment. An\n'
           'environment is defined as $\\mathcal { E } = ( \\mathcal { S } , \\mathcal { A } , \\mathcal { P } )$ , '
           'where A is the set\n'
           'of executable actions, S is the set of observable states, and $\\mathcal { P } ( s ^ { \\prime } \\mid s , '
           'a )$ is the\n'
           'transition dynamics. At time step t, the agent receives an observation $o _ { t } \\in \\mathcal { O }$ '
           'and produces an\n'
           'action $a _ { t } \\in \\mathcal A$ . Following the ReAct style formulation, the agent therefore selects '
           'an\n'
           'action $\\hat { a } _ { t } \\in \\hat { \\mathcal { A } }$ conditioned on its\n'
           'context $c _ { t } = \\left( o _ { 1 } , \\hat { a } _ { 1 } , \\dots , o _ { t - 1 } , \\hat { a } _ { t '
           '- 1 } , o _ { t } \\right)$\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\hat {a} _ {t} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot \\mid c _ {t}), \\qquad \\hat {a} _ {t} \\in \\hat {\\mathcal '
           '{A}}.\\tag{1}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'Executing $\\hat { a } _ { t } \\in \\mathcal A$ yields a new observation via the environment. The final '
           'trajectory\n'
           'is $\\tau = ( o _ { 1 } , \\hat { a } _ { 1 } , \\dots , o _ { T } , \\hat { a } _ { T } )$\n'
           '\n'
           'LLM Agent and Skill-Conditioned Execution. Let Q be the tasks set. We write $q \\in \\mathcal { Q }$ for '
           'sampling a task,\n'
           'and let $R ( \\tau , q ) \\in \\{ 0 , 1 \\}$ be a task-dependent success indicator. We model the LLM agent '
           'as a policy π\n'
           'that induces a trajectory distribution. Without external skills, the agent generates trajectories by '
           'direct reasoning:\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\tau \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | q), \\quad q \\in \\mathcal {Q}.\\tag{2}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'To reduce redundant exploration and improve task completion, we equip the agent with a skills\n'
           'library $\\mathcal { D } =$ $\\{ s _ { 1 } , \\dotsc , s _ { | \\mathcal { D } | } \\}$ and a skill '
           'retriever that recalls a\n'
           'set of relevant skills for the current task. Concretely, given $q \\in \\mathcal { Q } .$ a retrieval '
           'function (typically\n'
           'implemented via semanticsimilarity retrieval) $\\rho : \\mathcal { Q } 2 ^ { \\mathcal { D } }$ . returns '
           'a skill\n'
           'subset $S _ { q } \\ = \\ \\rho ( q ) , S _ { q } \\ \\subseteq \\mathcal { D }$ . The LLM agent then '
           'generates a trajectory\n'
           'by conditioning on the retrieved skill set:\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\tau^ {\\prime} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | \\mathcal {S} _ {q}, q), \\quad q \\in \\mathcal {Q}.\\tag{3}\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\n'
           'Our objective is to design the skills library D and the usage within π such that the expected success rate '
           'is improved:\n'
           '\n'
           '$$\n'
           '\\mathbb {E} _ {q \\in \\mathcal {Q}, \\tau^ {\\prime} \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | \\mathcal {S} _ {q}, q)} R '
           '(\\tau^ {\\prime}, q) > \\mathbb {E} _ {q \\in \\mathcal {Q}, \\tau \\sim \\pi (\\cdot | q)} R (\\tau , '
           'q).\\tag{4}\n'
           '$$',
